ParlaMint-SI_2015-01-27-SDZ7-Redna-04.ana.u1	Dear fellow Members and fellow Members, ladies and gentlemen! I'm about to proceed with the fourth session of the National Assembly. I am informed that today's meetings cannot be attended by the following Members: Mrs Ksenija Korenjak Kramar, Mrs Susana Lepi Šimenko, Mrs Andreja Potočnik, mag. Lilliana Kozlovich, Mrs Vojka Šergan by 16:00, Mrs Jelka Godec, Mr Primož Hainz, mag. Andrej Shircelj, Dr. Vinko Gorenak, mag. Matthew Tonin, Matjaž Hanžek, Mr Srečko Blažič, Miha Kordiš until 5 p.m., Mr Igor Zorčič from 9.30 a.m. to 13 p.m. Ange Logar. I say hello to everyone who's here! Your Honor! Today we mark the International Holocaust Memorial Day, so allow me to speak a few words about this before the meeting continues.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-01-27-SDZ7-Redna-04.ana.u2	Respectable citizens and citizens, fellow Members and fellow Members! Today, exactly 70 years after the long-awaited release of the notorious Nazi concentration camp Auschwitz, we mark the International Holocaust Memorial Day. More than 6 million Jews and hundreds of thousands of members of other ethnic and social groups, who have been described as obsolete in the idea of the Third Reich, died in this incomprehensible sea. More than 60,000 Slovenes and Slovenes were deported to the so-called death camps, more than six of them never returned, Slovenian Jews almost none. But today’s mark is not intended only for a gift to the victims, the survivors, and all those who sacrificed their lives from unselfish heroism to protect the lives of others. Today is also dedicated to historical, moral and political reflection, with which we must cultivate our collective understanding of the human abilities of good and evil. The Holocaust is a phenologically unique historical moment in which the state, the fundamental defender of individual rights, decided in principle, on purpose and on the basis of official policy, to wipe out all husband, wife and child solely on the basis of their belongings to a particular community. With its comprehensive, uncompromising and boundless murder, the Holocaust has dehumanized man and has shown that genocide is not only a murderous madness, but it is more – a policy that promises to drown foreign politics: one nation, one country, one truth. Unfortunately, the Holocaust, despite its uniqueness, is not the first crime of all crimes, which, in its preamble, also directly and unequivocally recognises the 1948 Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of the Crime of Genocide. Today, when we stand firmly in the 21st century with both legs, we see genocide in us as Janus' face of Western narratives of civilization and progress. Yet, not even tens of millions of victims of the rise of the national state and its imperialistic ambitions, nor millions of victims of the Holocaust, seem to have been sufficiently alert. Maybe we're learning too slowly. Maybe not enough. Maybe we still don't care. Philosopher Edmund Burke said that for the triumph of evil is enough for good people to do nothing. It's not just that people don't realize their constitutional power for power, it's enough to let them rule evil, not thinking. Cambodia, Rwanda, and Srebrenica are eloquent witnesses of this situation. Repeated is what we swore would be no more; the political utopia of genocide remains a dangerous temptation. But we can change that and we have to change it! With all that comes with humanity and humanity, we can face it only if we understand it. In the case of Holocaust and genocide, understanding of our moral and political duty with a view to confronting them is broader. We need to examine and question the links between inhumane atrocities and the burning social challenges and to understand that meaningful peace and coexistence cannot coexist with criminal inequalities that give birth to destructive extremes. This day therefore symbolizes not only the power of memory, but also the power and meaning of learning and understanding for a better future. Today we renew and consolidate our commitment to respect for human rights and human dignity, our commitment to respect for diversity, our commitment to a common understanding of freedom, equality and justice. It is our duty to transfer this common potential and this unrestrained power to the generations that are coming. Only in us will this preserve man and on earth mankind. Thank you.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-01-27-SDZ7-Redna-04.ana.u3	We're moving on to 12. On the basis of the information provided by the European Parliament and the Council on the implementation of the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union, the European Parliament and the Council are invited to take part in the implementation of the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union by the European Union, the European Parliament and the Council on the Functioning of the European Union, the European Parliament and the Council on the Functioning of the European Union, the Council and the Council on the The proposal for a declaration was submitted to the National Assembly by the Government of the Republic of Slovenia. I give the floor to the President of the Government, Dr Mir Cerar, to further explain the proposal for a declaration.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-01-27-SDZ7-Redna-04.ana.u4	Mr President, honourable Members and Members of Parliament! You have before you a declaration of guidelines for the functioning of Slovenia in the institutions of the European Union. This important document has been carefully considered at the meetings of the competent working bodies. Allow me to introduce myself to the key challenges facing the European Union and Slovenia in 2015. For the Union, 2015 may be a year of challenging political challenges, but also a period of high expectations. Responses to the tragic events that rocked France this month have expressed solidarity between citizens, peoples and countries of the European Union. At the solidarity meeting for unity in Paris, which I myself attended, we witnessed the extraordinary mobilisation of citizens and politicians in support of common values, in particular freedom of expression and the rule of law. Our job is to defend these values so that we can go together in difficult times. In the coming weeks, an effective response will be needed from all Member States, which, while respecting human rights and freedoms, must ensure the necessary security in Europe. Above all, we need to improve communication between countries and between parts of society and strengthen mutual trust and awareness that no religion, belief or ideology excuses terrorism. It is not only about increasing our security, but it is about common reflection on the causes, which also lead young people in particular to radicalisation and terrorist acts. To consider how to ensure understanding of diversity and successful integration into society. The European Union is a community of all. If we do not pay due attention to issues such as social inequality, lack of education, unemployment and alienation, such tragic and pointless events will be repeated in the future. At the same time, it is also important for the European Union to cooperate effectively with neighbouring and other countries. Although the assessment of the threat in Slovenia has not changed significantly, we are aware of the fragility of such a situation and therefore we want and need to be an active member in both security and other areas of cooperation, in particular between the European Union and the Western Balkan countries. A respected choir! The issues of freedom and security are, of course, not the only challenge we are facing in the European Union. The momentum of economic growth and employment has for some time required decisive action by both the European institutions and the governments of all Member States. If we want new jobs, we need to ensure sustainable economic growth. The latter can only be achieved through the right combination of responsible fiscal policy, forward-looking structural reforms and smart investment. In implementing this, we must be strong enough and, above all, credible to do what we do. Together we have achieved that Slovenia has successfully implemented a number of measures in the process of structural reforms and consolidation of public finances so far. They're already giving the first results. In doing so, we were also assisted by the so-called specific recommendations of the Commission, which some like to show as some kind of Brussels dictatorship. In fact, they're a long way from that. With the right approach, which we are already taking, we are being helped on the path of the country from the crisis, whether it be Slovenia or one of the other members of the European Union. We have embarked on this path, and with great efforts, particularly in recent months, we have made great progress, which is also evidence of relatively high economic growth. Evidence is also the renewed confidence of markets, as shown by improved credit ratings, and the fact that, despite recent fluctuations in financial markets, we achieve a record low return on Slovenian bonds, which makes our debt cheaper. We must not lose this renewed trust. If we want this growth to be long-term in order to bring more quality jobs, structural measures must continue. The government's objective for this year is clear: we want to reduce the deficit below 3% of the gross social product. We have also set up an appropriate budget balance for 2015. At the same time, with many other measures, we will take the first important steps towards concrete changes in public administration, health and, lastly, economic restructuring. In order to be more competitive, we are determined to make a marked improvement in the business environment, we have many young and pervasive entrepreneurs who need to be given a chance at home. We are known for our innovations, technological progress and research results, but we do not yet know how to make good use of them. We therefore want to reduce administrative burdens and simplify legislation in order to burden the small economy and pave the way for investment. This practice will also be advocated in the European institutions. We will also continue to privatise in the framework of the list of 15 companies. That is how we agreed and made a legal and political commitment at the highest level. Let me stress that privatisation is primarily aimed at improving corporate governance, opening up new markets and thus increasing competitiveness and growth. That's why we're looking for strategic partners. Of course, the full transparency of the procedure and the price are also important in choosing, as we want to reduce government debt with the buyer and finance measures for growth and a more social state. Promoting investment is a key element of economic growth. This year, special attention will be paid both in the European Union and at home. The Junker Investment Plan for Europe must simply be a success story for Europe and Slovenia. It is high time to move from many ideas to actions to start building, to update. Slovenia has identified 22 projects totalling EUR 9.2 billion in the investment plan so far. These are projects that must be implemented in one way or another – from the modernisation of the outdated rail network, the construction of flood protections, the upgrading of major road sections, to investments in the digital network and social infrastructure. This list is, of course, open to new projects. The Government is aware of the key importance of well-prepared projects and has therefore set up a working group to coordinate the preparation and monitoring of these investment projects. At the same time, we will note that clear criteria and funding mechanisms are agreed in the EU institutions when they consider legislative acts in this area, which will be equally accessible to all. It would be unfair and dangerous in its own terms if this instrument were to fail to help only larger and richer Member States. When we talk about investments, we must not forget that cohesion policy funds are still our main source of investment. According to the latest data, Slovenia received almost 2% of GDP in 2014 from the European budget. This means that we have received more than €633 million from the European budget than we have paid for it. In the last half of the year, after drawing from the 19th, we were able to turn to the 10th place among the members and, at the same time, successfully agree with the Commission on both a quality partnership agreement and an operational route. On her official visit days ago, the Commissioner for Regional Policy told me that she would promote Slovenia as an example of what can be done in such a short time. We can't lose this dynamic. We will insist on maximising cohesion funds as regards the successful completion of existing projects, as well as the successful and smart use of funding by 2020. We will also look for economic growth in trade agreements, particularly in the transatlantic trade and investment partnership, which is an important pillar in the newly defined transatlantic partnership. I am confident that the agreement will provide new opportunities for many Slovenian companies, but at the same time we will not allow the reduction of existing standards, in particular the reduction of the health, safety, nutrition, environmental and labour standards of the Union. Fear of an agreement is not entirely unfounded, but it is not insurmountable. We must overcome it with consistent transparency, through continuous dialogue with all those interested in progress in the negotiations and the examination of all aspects of the gradually emerging elements of the agreement. We must not rush into such important negotiations, nor at all at the expense of content. It is better to take more time if this proves necessary for a better agreement. At the same time, we need to know that in a globalised world, close partnerships are being sought everywhere. Ladies and gentlemen, for Slovenia it is also very important to strengthen the euro area, which is not only an economic monetary but also a political project. The success achieved so far in ensuring financial stability is due both to the actions of the European Central Bank and to the political will of the euro area Member States. Despite major steps in recent years in the establishment of the Economic and Monetary Union, the latter has not yet been completed. I expect that we will continue to discuss its further deepening in the coming month. In this context, solidarity and responsibility play an extremely important role for Slovenia. It is likely that a lot of energy will also need to be focused this year on relations with the Russian Federation, which since last year has been the most co-formed by the crisis in Ukraine. The European Union is united that the policy of sanctions can only be changed if the commitments made in the Minsk documents are met. The situation in eastern Ukraine has deteriorated significantly in recent days. Maintaining a principled stance on the need to respect the obligations of international law and the security framework and the territorial integrity and sovereignty of countries must remain at the heart of our continued action. The very changing nature of relations with Russia is one of the reasons for the adoption of measures to enhance the European Union's energy independence. I expect the European Commission to present a clear vision of the Energy Union in early February. A vision that includes all aspects of energy policy and will lead to a greener and cleaner Europe with secure energy supply and affordable energy. We also want the Energy Union to include ambitious climate policy. We are entering a key phase of international climate negotiations, which is expected to lead to a new global agreement at the end of this year and to a key stage in the preparation of new EU legislation to reduce greenhouse gas emissions beyond 2020. At the same time, both of them must make it possible to stop the rise in temperatures and lead to green growth with new jobs and to preserve the competitiveness of the European economy. The international conference in Paris must deliver ambitious results. Slovenia will continue to pay particular attention to the enlargement of the European Union to the Western Balkans and to supporting reforms in these countries. The fact is that enlargement policy is one of the greatest successes of the European Union and a key mechanism for ensuring stability, security and development on the European continent. This is particularly important for Slovenia, which borders this region and has important political and economic interests. Ladies and gentlemen, the year before us will be marked in one way or another by parliamentary elections in at least eight Member States. I would like to point out the recent elections in Greece and those coming in the United Kingdom. Elections are the foundation of democracy and people's will must be respected. I hope and believe that the final decisions of all actors will be prudent and that in these and other cases wisdom will prevail, that only together can we be stronger. Major challenges require strong political will, especially if we are dealing with dangerous forms of populism. I am convinced that limiting the freedoms on which the EU and its internal market are based will only lead to greater fragmentation and discriminatory treatment of its citizens. This has never been and must not become the purpose of European integration. We therefore need more courage to defend the positive aspects of European integration. We need a greater and more active focus of the Member States of the European Union on key dossiers leading to improved economic growth, competitiveness and quality jobs. This is particularly true for the territorial and demographic smaller countries, including Slovenia. Our fundamental strategic interest in an increasingly complex and competitive world is the preservation of a strong European Union and its institutions. This, of course, does not mean that we will not be constructively critical until they do. We must also not forget the policies which I may not have specifically mentioned today, but which nevertheless contribute significantly to the welfare and well-being of Slovenian and other European citizens. I have in mind safe food, self-sustainment with food, rights arising from public health, freedom of movement, free movement of information and so on. These are the specifics of Europe; the specifics of Europe, which Slovenia will always strive for. These specialities should remain and preserve Europe as a driving force for democracy and social and environmental awareness in the world. Dear Members and Members! The debate on the fundamentals and fundamental values of the European Union must also be revived in the search for economic growth and competitiveness through the prism of today. Also through dialogue with civil society, seek answers to a number of challenges, such as violence, youth unemployment, poverty and social exclusion. If we face our problems successfully at home, if we are united enough, open and credible in the European Union and the world, we will also be stronger. We must be aware of this in all areas of our operation in the institutions of the European Union. Thank you for your attention.

The Committee on European Union Affairs is at its 20th session. 1. 2015 As a competent committee, the proposal for a declaration submitted by 17. 11. 2014 The Government submitted to the National Assembly for its consideration. Within the time limit set for the proposed declaration, the amendments were tabled by the Members: Dr Simona Kustec Lipicer (SMC), Franc Jurša (Desus), Matjaž Han (SD) and Mr Jožef Horvat (NSi) and the Commission for National Communities. To the proposed amendments, the Government defined itself at the 20th meeting of the Committee, which was supported by the amendments by the Members of the Committee, Dr Simone Kustec Lipicer, Franca Jurša, Matjaž Hana and the Commission for the National Community, not by the amendment of Ambassador Jožef Horvat. Additional explanation The proposal for a declaration was made by Dragoljub Benčina, State Secretary at the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. Before the hearing, the rapporteurs presented the reports to the draft declaration by some of the working bodies of the National Assembly and the Commission of the State Council for International Relations and European Affairs. Otherwise, the Committee adopted reports from all the working bodies of the National Assembly. The proposal for a declaration was dealt with in terms of its competence and also supported by it. In the debate, MEPs highlighted, inter alia, the non-inclusion of the Investment Plan for Europe in the Proposal for a Declaration, the nomotechnic deficiencies regarding the period for which the Declaration is adopted, and the inclusion of the contents of the Digital Agenda for Europe. The representative of the Government made further clarifications on the questions raised. The Committee adopted the following amendments to the proposal for a declaration: several amendments to individual parts of the proposal for a declaration limiting the duration of its validity to 2015 and three amendments to Chapter Education, Youth, Culture, Sport, Culture and Audiovisual Policy. The specific content of the amendments adopted is also reflected in the report received. After the discussion and vote on the amendments, the Committee on European Union Affairs voted on all parts of the Proposal for a Declaration in the light of the Committee's competence. Eight members voted in favour and two opposed.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-01-27-SDZ7-Redna-04.ana.u7	I give the floor to the Chairman of the Committee on Foreign Policy, Mr Joseph Horvath, to present the report of the Committee on the Proposal for a Declaration and the additional report of the Committee on the completed proposal for a declaration.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-01-27-SDZ7-Redna-04.ana.u8	Thank you very much. Honourable President of the National Assembly, Honourable President of the Government, Ministerial Assembly, Honourable Colleagues and Colleagues! At its 12th meeting, on 16 January 2015, the Foreign Policy Committee considered the proposal for a declaration of guidelines for the functioning of the Republic of Slovenia in the institutions of the European Union from July 2014 to December 2015. Representatives of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and the Ministry of Defence and, of course, other representatives of the Government participated in the meeting of the Committee. Further explanation The proposal for a declaration was made by the State Secretary at the Ministry of Foreign Affairs of Dragoljub Benčina, who stressed that foreign policy is more difficult to tackle due to difficult foreseeable challenges and events and rapidly changing geopolitical situations. She said that the Ukrainian crisis had shown that the European Union needed a clearer vision for future cooperation with the Eastern Neighbourhood, taking into account the leadership for further economic and political integration with the Eastern Partnership countries. With regard to the Southern Mediterranean region, she stressed that it is facing more serious challenges, and it is therefore necessary to develop and support a common and effective neighbourhood policy in the form of a common platform for effective conflict resolution. According to Benchin, the proposal for a declaration also advocates a comprehensive approach to ensuring global peace and security and to respect human rights and freedoms and promotes a non-discriminatory approach to vulnerable groups. Mr Kopmajer, Vice-President of the Defence Committee, presented the report of the Defence Committee, which did not accept the amendments to the proposal for a declaration and supported the proposal for a declaration. The amendment's rapporteur, Tilen Bozič, the SMC Group, then presented the new text of the amendment, explaining the fundamental differences between the two, stressing the importance of transparency in the conclusion of the Agreement on the Transatlantic Partnership for Trade and Investment (TTIP), saying that it was at a stage where it was not yet available with sufficient information. The debate highlighted the gap between the proposal for a declaration and the actual situation in the area of neighbourhood and strengthening economic cooperation, the lack of public involvement in the development of transatlantic trade and economic agreements and the importance of ensuring security in the region and the world in view of past terrorist attacks in Paris. In the end, the question was raised whether the Government had already addressed the projections of the contribution of TTIP to the Slovenian economy. In doing so, the Secretary of State explained that the projections were being prepared, followed by an analysis which would form the basis for the formation of the Government's position. This position will also be presented in the National Assembly. After the discussion, the Committee decided on the newly proposed amendment of a qualified proposer and adopted the following amendment by thirteen votes and one against: In Chapter External Affairs and Defence, the EU chapter as a global actor and relations with strategic partners, the third and fourth paragraphs amend and the text is given in writing. Finally, the Committee adopted, with eleven votes against and one against, the opinion: The Committee on Foreign Policy, as a competent working body, considered, in the light of its competence, the Proposal for a Declaration on Guidelines for the Functioning of the Republic of Slovenia in the European Union institution from July 2014 to December 2015 to Chapter External Affairs and Defence, adopted the amendment in the EU chapter as a global actor and relations with strategic partners. Mr President, colleagues and colleagues! Allow me to present an additional report. At its 12th urgent meeting on 23 January 2015, the Foreign Policy Committee considered the proposed amendment of qualified applicants under Article 154(4) of the Rules of Procedure. Representatives of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs participated in the committee. A further explanation of the amendment was given by the leader of the Group of Ambassadors, Associated Left colleague Luka Mesec. The State Secretary at the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, Dragoljub Benčič, said that the negotiations were still under way and the Committee had voted on the proposed amendment and had not adopted the amendment. He voted with only one vote in favour and 13 votes against. Thank you very much for your attention.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-01-27-SDZ7-Redna-04.ana.u9	I give the floor to the representatives of the parliamentary groups to present their views. The New Slovenia Group – Christian Democrats, Mr. Joseph Horvat, has the floor.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-01-27-SDZ7-Redna-04.ana.u10	Thank you very much, Mr. President. Honourable Prime Minister of the Government, Honourable Council of Ministers, dear colleagues and colleagues! Let me first, on behalf of the New Slovenia Group of Christian Democrats, thank the President of the National Assembly for reminding us today, on behalf of all of us, of the victims of the Holocaust. Colleagues and colleagues, today we are talking about a declaration on the direction and functioning of the Republic of Slovenia in the institutions of the European Union for the period July 2014 to December 2015, i.e. for a period of half a year ago. It would make sense to consider the declaration only for the future period and, in return, to consider a larger report on the functioning of the Republic of Slovenia in the institutions of the European Union. So much for formality. The reasons are, in fact, known to be why such a delay. We have also drawn attention to these formalities at the time of consideration of the declaration on the competent working bodies. On the content, I would like to put an end to the introductory reflection on the better functioning of the European Union and on the more effective cooperation and exchange of mutual information between the European Union and Slovenia, and in particular on the active participation of the Slovenian Government in the functioning of the institutions of the European Union. In the past, it has been noted that Slovenia has not been sufficiently active in the institutions of the European Union, particularly at the meetings of individual Councils of the European Union. It was noted that there was no Slovenian opinion or Slovenian vote. Our ministers don't go to council meetings. This was said at the beginning of this year, on 5 January, at the consultation of Slovenian diplomacy. In the past, Mr Rado Genorio has challenged the poor participation of Slovenian ministers in ministerial councils. Lately, this statistics have improved visibly and we want to keep it that way and welcome it. It is true, too, that the Members of the New Slovenia – Christian Democrats – are following the compositions of delegations at the meetings of the Council of the European Union and we note that the participation of ministers has been good in recent times. We would also like some Member States of the European Union to implement the practice in the National Assembly, to have the President of the Government present before the European Council or before the competent committee, and to report to the National Assembly or at least to the competent committee after the European Council. This is every time a key decision at EU level, which, of course, also tanges the Republic of Slovenia. A number of Member States of the European Union have an exemplary exchange of information with the institutions of the European Union. The New Slovenia Group expects that when we amend the law on cooperation between the Government and the National Assembly in matters of the European Union, we will also write this in law. Therefore, better cooperation in the spirit of the Treaty of Lisbon, which gives national parliaments more power, is more powerful. We have repeatedly said that national parliaments are an integral part of the European parliamentary system. The second area to be highlighted is the European Union 2020 strategy and the European Semester. Within the 2020 strategy, we believe that there is not enough will to implement the Digital Agenda for Europe. The Digital Agenda for Europe is one of the most important components of the Europe 2020 strategy. ICT companies, information and communication technologies, are today not only in Europe, but also in the world, the fastest growing companies. We must be aware that Slovenia has enormous potential in this area, we have good ICT engineers, but we do not provide the right business environment for the implementation of this digital agenda. We also hear this from some government representatives. Due to the unincentived business environment, ICT companies, especially medium and small businesses, and young ICT engineers are moving abroad, and we are losing jobs and intellectual potential. Colleagues and colleagues, the world will be digital, Europe will be digital, and Slovenia will be digital. The one who won't be digital won't be digital. The third package, which we must mention in the declaration, is not explicitly defined as an area to be regulated this year, but tax discrimination against migrant workers. We are talking about about about 15,000, maybe 20,000 Slovenian workers who emigrate daily or weekly from Pomurje, Styria, Koroška and other parts of Slovenia mainly to Austria and are discriminated against in the payment of income according to tax residence. In fact, in Austria in the same factory, they may be doing the same work with the same machine, the same operation of two workers; one is our resident, the other is an Austrian resident. For the same work you get, clearly, the same gross salary, and the net salary is very different. We're not going to trouble the Income Act here, we're not going to trouble the constitutional decisions. That's not a problem. The problem is, in fact, that the European Commission started dealing with at the beginning of last year, and that is tax discrimination. It is true that we do not have the same tax system in the Member States, but in some way this problem should be solved or affected workers – so at least they think they are affected – these 18 000 – have to be given clear answers by the Government. Ladies and gentlemen, the New Slovenia Group of Members - Christian Democrats We will not oppose the proposal for a declaration.

ParlaMint-SI_2015-01-27-SDZ7-Redna-04.ana.u12	Thank you so much for saying that, Mr. President. Dear Mr President of the Government, distinguished Minister, Ministers, Colleagues and colleagues! The proposal for a declaration of guidelines for the functioning of the Republic of Slovenia in the institutions of the European Union enjoys our support. We agree with the priorities of guidance, such as strengthening the Economic and Monetary Union, improving the business environment and economic growth, creating an energy union with an appropriate and human-friendly climate policy, promoting better quality and biofriendly food, and, last but not least, the European Union's visible role in addressing security, humanitarian, migration and other current challenges. The proposal for a declaration was also addressed by the Commission for the National Communities of the National Assembly. In the debate, we focused on the area of human rights with particular emphasis on the direct and direct impact of such documents on the conservation and development of national communities. Although the competent organisations of the Hungarian and Italian communities support the proposal for a declaration, it was also critically pointed out in the debate that the guidelines for the proposed declaration take into account very high standards, which is correct and which is still formally applicable to national policy in Slovenia, but are not consistently implemented or enforced in practice. While the guidelines for the declaration we are receiving today are in force within the framework of the functioning of the Republic of Slovenia in the institutions of the European Union, we believe that there is always a need to draw attention to the gap between formal standards and actual practice. The proper formality of this content in the documents, as well as the proposed declaration, is not sufficient, and it is therefore expected that matters will actually change for the better. In the section Education, Youth, Culture, Sport and Sub-section Culture and Audiovisual Policy, we proposed an amendment which, compared to the original text, more clearly and bindingly emphasises Slovenia's commitment to the protection and promotion of cultural and linguistic diversity and the development of intercultural dialogue and the development of intercultural competences in this field. This is in conjunction with the Treaty of Lisbon, which stipulates that the European Union respects its rich cultural and linguistic diversity and cares for the protection and development of European cultural heritage. The proposed supplement, which also benefited from the support of the Ministry of Culture, was supported by the competent committee and is thus an integral part of the proposed text. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-01-27-SDZ7-Redna-04.ana.u13	The word is given by the SMC Group, for which Dr. Simona Kustec Lipicer.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-01-27-SDZ7-Redna-04.ana.u14	Dear President of the National Assembly, distinguished ministerial team, colleagues and colleagues! The European Union is now one of the world's leading regions, both in the humanist and social spheres, and especially in the economic and financial sphere. The European Union's development policies and financial resources are those that can provide Member States with important support and impetus in building their national development capacities, if that is what these countries, of course, want to do and can benefit from. The European Union's GDP in the last decade, since its member is also Slovenia, amounts to around 12 trillion euros annually. In 2012, for example, the European Union of Slovenia allocated a total of EUR 932 million in financial resources, representing 2.66 per cent of Slovenia's gross social product. During the same year, Slovenia contributed EUR 333.5 million and 0.95 million of its gross social product to the European Union budget. Our country's entry into the monetary union in 2007 has only increased the role of the European Union in our country, particularly through the greater and more direct impact that the institutions of the European Union have taken on in domestic policy. In the current context of the economic and financial crisis, the impact of the European Union institution through various political mechanisms, such as the European Semester and budgetary control, has further strengthened, and at the same time potentially has also strengthened the power of our country or of the Member States of the Monetary Union, as it has given them the opportunity to represent their national interests, the interests of their country and people through co-forming policies, if they know and want to take advantage of it. In the SMC Group, we believe that the country's orientation into the international environment is one of the pillars of our country's further development. We also believe that it is the European Union that represents an important partner with which these development potentials can be co-examined and co-formed if we only participate in these processes in a responsible and active manner. The proposed declaration confirms Slovenia's commitment to European values and objectives, as already defined in the Treaty on European Union and the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union. The content of the declaration of guidelines for the functioning of the Republic of Slovenia in the institutions of the European Union during the period from July 2014 to December 2015 is, in the light of everything mentioned, understood as the starting policy document not only the political orientations of the European Union, but in particular the central guidelines of our country in the economic, monetary, sustainable and development, political and wider social environment of the European Union. From the content of the declaration, clear political commitments after dialogue and partnership can be seen both between the political institutions of the country and the institutions of the European Union, the civil society groups concerned and the political institutions within the country. In the case of the latter, particular attention is also paid to the commitments, relations and responsibilities between the executive and legislative branches of authorities within our country or each country in co-forming the European Union's policy. The adoption of common policy guidelines and decisions at European Union level after adoption becomes binding for Member States. It is precisely this fact that plays the role of national parliaments as the central political institution through which people must express and represent the will of people in policy-making and adoption processes. Parliaments play a central role in this regard, ensuring the implementation of the fundamental principles of democracy, the legitimate and responsible functioning of the authorities and, for these reasons, in presenting the SMC Group's position on the proposal for a declaration of guidelines for the functioning of the Republic of Slovenia in the EU institutions, I would like to touch on the roles and competences of the National Assembly of the Republic of Slovenia. The Slovenian National Assembly takes an extremely important place in EU affairs, since, like in Germany, France, Sweden, Austria, Finland, Hungary, the role of Parliament is constitutionally defined in European Union matters. The National Assembly shall be given the powers of the supervisor or the escort to keep the Government informed of political activities at EU level, as well as of the co-founder of national substantive positions to be taken into account by the Government on behalf of the State in its further operation at European Union level. The relationship between the National Assembly and the Government is further governed by the Law on Cooperation between the National Assembly and the Government in matters of the European Union, which must be adopted by the two-thirds quorum of the Members present. Furthermore, these relations in detail are also defined within the Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly of the Republic of Slovenia. The role of the national Parliament is therefore understood as crucial in both the substantive and the procedural aspects of Slovenia. In the process, it is expected that Parliament will participate formally, through the work of its home labour bodies and also in plenary meetings, as is the case today, in co-forming our national positions and the role of our country in the European Union, as provided for by the letters of law. In terms of content and perspective, in particular, he will identify and create concrete substantive objectives and actions through his past good experience and practices and clear programming visions and the pursuit of will and the needs of people together with the Government. To date, monitoring the functioning of the National Assembly in EU matters, as disclosed by, for example, the data of the State Assembly's Research and Documentation Sector in a procedural sense, shows that the work of the National Assembly has been well done. The number of meetings and cases dealt with has continued to be high since accession to the EU. Unfortunately, we cannot confirm this with the same certainty in the event of the actual substantive involvement of the National Assembly in the process of co-forming EU policy. In addition to the day-to-day parliamentary experience, they also point out the findings of international comparative studies, which, unlike ministries and the role of national civil servants, in Slovenia do not recognize at all as an relevant substantive co-founder of national positions at EU level. The first, as well as the constitutional, legal and commercially defined cooperation between the Government and Parliament in this context, is recognised as negligiblely irrelevant. The points raised point to a number of fundamental problems that our country faces and must also reconsider if it wants to take its commitment after open and constructive action in the EU to the future. The general absence of the National Assembly from these processes, which is most often demonstrated in practice by the fact that the EU's parent working body is given a hearing or better said formal confirmation in a package, literally overnight, directly to the meeting table, not only by depriving Parliament in practice of the competence to co-decision in EU matters, but by the fateful establishment of the fundamental principles of a democratic, transparent and, above all, in development and in public good time, a well-focused and well-informed political process at the level of the country. Of course, we welcome and support the SMC Group today's declaration because of our programme commitments and our other belief in its pro-active orientation, while at the same time aware of the political responsibilities that we bear as Members in the processes described to our citizens, so we also bear our call here to improve the practice so far and, in future, in a timely and substantive manner, to work together to address such documents, which should have been dealt with at least a year ago and not only at a time when a third of the content relating to the period from July 2014 to January 2015 has been formally considered, I believe that today's formal approval of this document has taken place. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2015-01-27-SDZ7-Redna-04.ana.u19	The word is given by the Group of Members of the United Left, for her Luka Moon.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-01-27-SDZ7-Redna-04.ana.u20	Dear Mr President, the honourable Council of Ministers, distinguished colleagues and colleagues, thank you for the floor. There are only two things I'd like to say about the declaration. Firstly, this declaration, with its high-flying objectives, is nothing new and strongly reminds us of the two seriously failed Lisbon Strategy, which had similar high-flying objectives of economic growth, development, job creation, digital agendas, knowledge society and so on, and we have seen for the past ten years that we are longer than these objectives, but the response of the Government and the European Commission is simple to extend the deadlines for them. The second point to be pointed out is that Slovenia's position on this declaration reflects the surviving ideologies that even institutions such as the IMF are developing today. So obviously we didn't learn anything from the crisis. However, I will spend a few minutes on the problem of the part which I think is most harmful in the short term, this is support for the free trade agreements of TISA, CETA and TTIP as defined in this declaration. Here Slovenia has a very naive position because it advocates the early conclusion of these agreements for an unknown reason. naive and illogical — Why? Firstly, even an economic study commissioned by the Ministry of Economic Development and Technology at the Centre for Business Excellence of the Faculty of Economics notes that: for the first time, the impact on Slovenian GDP by these agreements will be negative; for the second time, that there is probably a risk of job cuts in Slovenia; for the third time, that these agreements do not meet Slovenia's own economic interests, for example, in the field of automotive industry, pharmacy and generic medicines, which are key sectors for the Slovenian economy. Why – because in all these cases competition will get tighter and our companies will have problems. Last but not least, this same study notes that the extremely problematic lack of knowledge of Slovenian companies on the TTIP negotiations can lead potentially to the unpreparedness of Slovenian companies to tight competition and can therefore lead to additional secondary negative effects on the Slovenian economy. Studies by foreign independent institutions and even the European Commission's position in the case of jobs make it clear that we are at risk of losing, even between 400 000 and a million 100,000 jobs in Europe. So we're going to lose about as many jobs as Juncker's strategy is supposed to be able to achieve if it ever miraculously manages to make 300 billion for investment. In order to support this further, the European Commission warned itself before the first round of negotiations that the TTIP would likely bring about a long-term and extensive migration of European jobs, as companies will encourage them to turn to products and services from those US federal countries where lower labour standards are in place than in Europe and trade rights are almost non-existent. They already have that kind of experience in the United States. In fact, similar to the NAFTA free trade agreement concluded between the USA, Canada and Mexico in 1994, it similarly promised the creation of new jobs and turned out to be the opposite. The Washington Economic Policy Institute has shown that NAFTA has actually lost more than a million jobs during the first 12 years of existence in the United States and has also significantly reduced the wages of several million additional workers. These are the consequences of free trade agreements if they are accepted with countries where lower labour and social standards apply. Therefore, TTIP, CETA and TISA are an exceptional threat to the Slovenian economy and to labour rights in general. Another issue which is extremely problematic in these agreements is the investor-country dispute resolution mechanism, the so-called ISDS mechanism. This mechanism means that an arbitral tribunal will be established in which corporations investing in a country can sue that country if they realise that any measures have an impact on their income or profits. Now, if I make a specific case. If, for example, some foreign insurance company bought the Triglav Insurance Company, if Slovenia, as we have recently done, accepted the insurance tax, this foreign insurance company could now sue this arbitral tribunal of the state of Slovenia for the loss of income. Of course, it doesn't apply the other way around. And the state can't sue corporations. In short, this ISDS arbitration mechanism will definitively push or subordinate States to the interests of international capital and is also an additional matter which the Republic of Slovenia should necessarily question in this agreement or this declaration. And the third thing. It should be noted that consumer protection, or the very fact that the TISA, CETA and TTIP agreements are not simply free trade agreements, since customs between the US and the European Union are already minimal in most cases, but are in fact the removal of administrative barriers, with administrative barriers usually representing environmental, social and labour standards established for such and other reasons of protection or of workers, or consumers, or the environment. This is called administrative obstacles, as is often the case with us, but in fact this means, if I illustrate the case of the food industry, the following. In the United States, for example, more than 70% of all processed foods currently sold in stores contain genetically modified ingredients. This has not occurred in Europe because of the resistance of Member States and the resistance of European parliamentarians, but it is clear, and this is confirmed by John Hillary's study, which has been following this negotiations since the very beginning, that American companies are lobbying heavily, that legislation on genetically modified food will change in Europe and that American companies could also start selling genetically modified organisms to these markets. That's another thing. For example, more than 90% of American beef is produced using bovine growth hormone, which some studies have found to be related to human cancer. As a result, restrictions on imports of beef have been in place in Europe since 1988. Again: the US government and the American food industry are lobbying within the negotiations on these agreements to remove this administrative obstacle. And for the third time, American chicken and turkey meat producers routinely process the birds before selling to consumers with chlorine. This process was also banned in Europe for a reason in 1997 because it is harmful to health. It is also intended to remove this administrative obstacle through free trade agreements. In short, these free trade agreements between Europe and the United States are anti-democratic because countries will subject themselves to final international capital, international capital will even have an adversarial right if countries influence its revenues in one way or another, jobs will be destroyed because free trade agreements are adopted with countries where they have significantly lower labour and environmental standards, and consumer protection will be removed by means of administrative barriers at a substantially lower level than it is now. In the United Left, we therefore, of course, oppose the adoption of these trade agreements, while also rejecting the views expressed by the Republic of Slovenia in this declaration because they are simply harmful. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2015-01-27-SDZ7-Redna-04.ana.u30	Thank you for your word. Dear Members and Members of the Council of Ministers! The declaration, especially in this part, seems good to me. Of course, this is not the only document that will guide us within the European Union. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2015-01-27-SDZ7-Redna-04.ana.u46	Thank you for your word. I would like to explain only the amendment tabled by the United Left coalition and the secret or trade agreements. That is why Slovenia supports the conclusion of the Agreement to the Transatlantic Partnership for Trade and Investment of TTIP, taking into account the preservation of environmental, social, labour, health and other standards, replaces by a stricter formulation, which reads as follows, "Slovenia supports the conclusion of all those agreements which, on the basis of economic analysis and impact assessments on society and the environment, bring economic growth and a general increase in social well-being", and to add, "that the CETA Agreement is now in the latest version", in the current form does not provide positive effects on society and the environment and does not bring economic growth and social well-being", which also shows studies on this agreement. Why we suggested this, I explained it earlier. Firstly, because studies show that jobs will be lost in Slovenia as a result. Secondly, that these agreements do not embody, do not take into account the economic interests of this country and, thirdly, that the effects on economic growth will be negative. Next. Studies at European Union level show that these trade agreements, namely TTIP and CETE, can cause up to one million jobs to be lost in Europe and the United States. Next. The impact on consumer protection and the protection of social rights will be negative. And last time, the most problematic in the whole story is the ISDS mechanism, which means the investor protection mechanism, according to which, as I explained earlier, state investors can sue if countries choose to amend legislation for such or other reasons. There are precedents for this in the world. For example, the Swedish energy company Vattenfall is currently suing the German government for €3.7 billion simply because, following the Fukushima disaster, Germany decided to start gradually abandoning nuclear energy. In Australia, we currently have a scandal with the tobacco company Philip Morris, who is suing the Australian government because it has decided, as part of its public health policy, that tobacco dealers must sell cigarettes in a single package. The same company, Philips Morris, had already received a similar lawsuit in Uruguay where they passed a law that health warnings should cover 80% of the area of a pack of cigarettes. In fact, similar matters have already received a worldwide epidemic ratio. Now known data have been received by corporations up to over 500 procedures against 95 countries, of which more than 400 in the last ten years. The agreements that Slovenia would support with this declaration or give a green light for their further adoption would put it at the level of some standard. Here I can only agree with Professor John Hilary, who found in his study that we can rightly understand that these agreements do not involve negotiations between two trading partners and competitors, but rather an attempt by transnational corporations to open new markets on both sides of the Atlantic and the existing ones subject to deregulation. And as it concludes, these documents are documents for deregulation, for assault on jobs and for the end of democracy. Thank you.

Again, I am returning to this problem that it will be very difficult to determine what the proposal for a specific sentence is. Let me conclude that the position is undoubtedly my and the government's own, that we must be very critical of totalitarian ideas. That we must understand that these are ideas that do not belong to a democratic society, but that are uncompromising persecution of some symbols that have a multi-layered history that are not explicitly defined, but that would mean a shift from this democracy, because also uncompromising persecution of certain symbols is a characteristic of regimes that are not democratic. Therefore, I believe that such a legal arrangement as proposed can actually lead in the opposite direction that we all want together. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2015-01-27-SDZ7-Redna-04.ana.u122	Madam President, thank you for the floor. Colleagues and colleagues! The media are one of the foundations of any democratically regulated society that ensures the dissemination of information as an important condition for its development. The operation of the media also ensures the dissemination of the Slovenian word and helps to foster patriotism and a positive attitude towards their own country and language. In the absence of domestic creativity, the media become a promoter of foreign cultural contexts, a generator of the deterioration of national identity, self-image and finally the sovereignty of the state and nation. Slovenian music shares at commercial radio stations barely reach the current legal obligation, a limit of 20% of the broadcasting time, which is dedicated to Slovenian music. To a large extent, it is an abuse of currently-lawed music quotas, as Slovenian music is played in more than 90% during the night. Instead of the programming diversity and wide presentation of all music types of domestic music within these percentages, we listen to the repetition of the same musical performances for the widest range of listeners. Following the claim of the Slovenian Music Union, Slovenian music creativity and discography were also in serious crisis, both substantive and material. International comparisons show that Slovenia has relatively poorly taken care of its own musical creativity with the prescribed percentages compared to other European countries. Some European countries with the most powerful European discographic industries do not need legal quotas, and some countries with larger music markets, such as Slovenia, do not require much more legally required minimum levels of national music. The proposed changes would limit such abuses and allow the development of quality Slovenian music of different types. In the field of audiovisual works, too, we can see that the requirement to record and verify the fulfilment of the prescribed quotas for audiovisual works in the current legislation is impossible to implement in practice. Due to the lack of accurate data, effective controls are disabled and, consequently, there is a risk that the issuer does not respect the prescribed quotas, which leads to poor audiovisual production. With the proposed more precise definitions, we also want to limit abuses in this area. The proposal for amendments and the amendment of the law will indirectly encourage the creativity of Slovenian musicians as well as audiovisual creators. Due to the greater share of broadcasting time available for Slovenian music, it will be possible to present a larger number of Slovenian musicians and a larger number of Slovenian musical types, resulting in a greater variety of such programming content and higher quality of domestic artists, and resulting in new jobs. This will also encourage the development of Slovenian music creativity as a promising economic sector in Slovenia and indirectly in the European Union. The proposal to amend the law also limits the abuse of currently-lawed quotas, since the quota is legal at two intervals instead of a daily one. Such a quality and time-consuming quota is also one of the key protections for uninterrupted music creation in the Republic of Slovenia.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-01-27-SDZ7-Redna-04.ana.u123	Thank you. For an introductory explanation, I give the floor to the representative of the Government of Ms Mag. Juliani Bizjak Mlakar, Minister of Culture. Here you go.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-01-27-SDZ7-Redna-04.ana.u124	Thank you very much, Madam Vice President. Dear Members and Members! The Government of the Republic of Slovenia discussed the proposal for a law amending and supplementing the Law on Media at its meeting on 22 January this year. She decided to give a negative opinion on this proposal. Let us take a closer look at the reasons for the rejection. The proposal for the amendment does not comply with the Resolution on the National Programme for Culture 2014-2017 adopted by the National Assembly of the Republic of Slovenia on 27. 11. 2013 and foresees a comprehensive revision of media legislation on the basis of a pre-prepared media strategy. The proposal for a new law is prepared without prior analysis and without a comprehensive solution to the problems, which is, inter alia, the purpose of the strategy. It only proposes individual partial changes to the existing law, and the consequences are not examined. It brings a new controversial definition of audiovisual works. The provisions on the prescribed proportion of Slovenian music and Slovenian audiovisual works are being amended and the consequences of this proposal have not been examined. This part refers in particular to RTV Slovenia. A register of independent producers is being introduced, but the provisions governing this area are very weak. The law has many other shortcomings. The additional problem is that there was no professional and other interested public involved in the consideration of the proposal for a new law, and above all, the proposal is without analysis of the consequences of the proposals that the professional public draws attention to, which, due to the lack of analysis, cannot be either contradicted or confirmed. The revision of media legislation requires the preparation and adoption of a national strategic document which will provide guidance for the development of the media landscape in the Republic of Slovenia on the basis of an analysis of the existing situation. The need for a strategic document is also confirmed by the fact that all attempts to adopt new media legislation so far have failed. A national strategy is needed in this area, which would clearly define public interest in the field of media. In the case of partial proposals such as those under consideration, the consequences of the proposals must be examined. The area of ensuring the prescribed shares of Slovenian music in radio and television programmes, or Slovenian audiovisual parts of television programmes, is certainly necessary to renovate, and in this work we agree with the efforts of the proposers of the amendment to the law. The public interest, followed by the Government and the Ministry of Culture, is, as much quality Slovenian music as possible at the time of maximum listening and as many quality Slovenian audiovisual works as possible during the time of maximum viewing. However, on the question of whether these parts are sufficient, the drafter does not have analyses, data and also unsatisfactory arrangements. However, certain data and warnings show that serious problems may arise in providing sufficient quantities of quality content. Even at the Ministry, we are not satisfied that some radio houses use the current law in order to broadcast the prescribed parts of Slovenian music only at night and that in finding listeners or viewers, the quality of content deteriorates. However, the proposed amendment does not fully address and would cause many other problems. With the law under study, it could cause harm to those who have so far made the most efforts for national interest. Many questions the applicant does not have satisfactory answers and evidence. The proposal for a new law, which is extremely short, contains only six articles, the areas under consideration are not fully regulated, as it does not offer any solutions to key problems, but in areas where it proposes changes, these are unexamined and such that the current situation may not deteriorate too well. The proposal for the amendment therefore has a number of shortcomings, which we will hear more about today and which are the reason for the rejection of the position of the Government of the Republic of Slovenia and the Ministry of Culture of the Republic of Slovenia. Regardless of the above, we thank the proposers for their good intentions and constructive proposals in terms of direction towards increasing the prescribed share of Slovenian production that we will be [[…]] [[sign for the end of the debate]] Amendments. ParlaMint-SI_2015-01-27-SDZ7-Redna-04.ana.u126	Madam President, Madam Minister, honourable Members and Members of Parliament! Let me begin by saying that we will not support the news of the Media Law proposed by SDS in the Parliamentary Group of the Mir Cerar Party. We note, however, that the SDS has become self-critical because it is clear that part of the Media Act adopted by the Government of Janez Janša in 2006 does not produce satisfactory results. At the same time, it should be noted that the law is also problematic in other areas and deserves comprehensive renovations, not only in the four articles that the SDS would now like to amend and relate to the definition and play of audiovisual works and the mandatory quotas for the production of Slovenian music or the production of Slovenian creators and creators. We believe that it is high time, in accordance with the National Programme for Culture, to complete an analysis of the media landscape, which has already been made to a considerable extent, and to adopt the Strategy of the Republic of Slovenia in the field of media on the basis of it. The adoption of partial changes in system legislation, which measure only the increase in mandatory production quotas and which do not systematically cover both the problems of promoting quality works and the performance of audiovisual works, seems inappropriate to us, especially since, given the accompanying material submitted, it seems that the proposed legislative changes not only do not have the basis in the analysis of the area to improve but are written without the basic knowledge of the actual situation, which I will explain further. In the SMC Group, we find the proposed novelties problematic for the following reasons. The amendment to Article 68(1) of the novel is not appropriate because it defines audiovisual works as works which are in any way expressed as individual creations in the field of literature, science and art and has been written for them by a synopsis or scenario, meaning that other audiovisual creations other than literature, science or art are not audiovisual works. What, for example, is a documentary on a particular social issue? The animated film is another example, which is not sufficiently covered by the definition of the concept. There's no script for him, but there's no storyboard. Similarly, video clips, talk shows, television shows, and so on remain out of definition. The proposal thus completely ignores or overruns two laws defining audiovisual work, the Slovenian Film Centre Act and the Copyright and Related Rights Act. As a result, the uncoordinated definition of audiovisual work would create chaos in the exercise of rights in public tendering for audiovisual works. Article 2 has a partially justified basis in the sense that Slovenian music should also be played during the daytime and not only mostly in the nighttime, which unfortunately is now happening. We agree that this should be supplemented or rectified in order to prevent the act from being used for the purpose of the law following the amendment of the Media Law. However, it is a problematic provision in the third paragraph, requiring the proportion of productions of Slovenian creators under 5 years of age, because it is not clear whether there are enough quality works that are under 5 years of age and would meet this requirement. On the other hand, as has already been said, there is no provision in terms of targeted promotion of the performance of quality works by new young creators. With regard to the provision relating to mandatory broadcasting quotas, the Public Institute of RTV Slovenia notes, quote: "The implementation of the proposed changes in the media landscape would lead to real devastation and would completely undermine the work of media creators." Also: "It is impossible to attach to the applicant that Slovenian music, in particular that has been created in the last 5 years, is the criterion that is supposed to nurture and maintain a high level of Slovenian language." If we move on to Article 3 of the novel, we see that the SDS proposes to increase the quota of Slovenian audiovisual parts of each TV programme. This seems questionable, however, as to how the number of shares will be monitored when the criterion is laid down to count the premiere playback and the first repetition for broadcasts over 5 years of age and one performance within 5 years. Abuses appear to be even more frequent if the eligibility criteria for the works presented appear to be exacerbated in such a rather complex way. Article 4 of the proposed novel is most questionable of all, since the implementation of the legal requirement at the lower threshold of 600 hours of prime Slovenian audiovisual works on RTV Slovenia and at least 150 hours of prime Slovenian audiovisual works by independent producers is impossible. It is completely inconsistent with the budgetary capabilities and realistic financial frameworks in which Slovenian film or audiovisual production takes place. For the realisation of film and audiovisual works, the Ministry of Culture has provided around €3 million per year for several years, which is enough to produce a maximum of 5 films and a maximum of 15 audiovisual works. In addition, if we add up to 1.7 million euros that RTV Slovenia has to devote to cinematographic films after the obligation of the Slovenian Film Centre Act, we can add up to a maximum of 2 feature films and a few short documentarys each year, i.e. about 5 hours of the premiere program. So it is clear that in Slovenia public money is enough for a few more than 24 hours program of independent producers. Where to get the extra money, it's not quite clear. The proposal says that the novel should not have a financial impact on the budget, which is, of course, far from the truth. In its response to the proposed amendment, RTV Slovenia emphasises that an additional EUR 20 million would be needed to achieve the quotas provided for in the novel. This is the sum equivalent to the annual annual annual programming cost of RTV Slovenia. This, for national television, Radiotelevision, means an impossible dilemma during salary cuts, read the dismissal of employees or the increase in RTV contributions. It is clear that in the current economic situation, it is impossible to increase RTV's contribution to this end. It is also not possible to expect an increase in the income of RTV Slovenia from selling the advertising space for a short period of time. So it's a dead end. The key point of the SMC Group is that the interest of Slovenian creators and performers, such as musicians, film artists and so on, is also important, and it is equally important to ensure the real possibilities of implementing legal provisions relating to distributors or media, in the case of musicians or filmmakers, in particular radio or TV programmes. We believe that both creators and producers as well as quality media landscape are important, that we are not even talking about editorial freedom and autonomy. We are of the opinion that if a State introduces a restrictive measure to the economy and increases the quotas for the compulsory share of Slovenian production, then an incentive measure must also be offered. The government has promised that there will be no additional burden on the economy. It would also be necessary to consider limiting measures in order, for example, to change the financing of the media in such a way as to support media content that is developing well-supported by Slovenian music and audiovisual production. However, the current law, based on politically balanced media financing, does not allow this. However, it allows, for example, a certain medium, the majority owner of which is one of the parliamentary parties, to receive money from the media tender. In the opinion of the SMC Group, both quality Slovenian production and editorial policy should be rewarded or financially stimulated, which takes account of this type of production and is included in the series. Just setting quotas without incentives is undoubtedly a lack of preconceived action with unforeseeable consequences, but it is also too much of an intervention in the freedom of professional judgement of each editor. If we draw the line, we can say that the proposed novel would bring about changes only in one area of the Media Law, but not systematically, without a real or comprehensive view of the actual situation. Its adoption would have many harmful financial and legal consequences. That's why we're not going to support the News of the Media Act. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2015-01-27-SDZ7-Redna-04.ana.u130	Dear Vice-President, distinguished Minister with co-workers, honourable Members and Members of Parliament have a very nice greeting to all! Allow me to present to you the position of the Desus Group on the Proposal for a Law on Amendments and Supplements to the Media Act. The substance of the proposed amendment to the Media Law is the amendment to the current 86th article, which significantly increases the share of broadcasting time available for music of Slovenian origin. The law now in force stipulates that at least 20% of all daily music of each radio and television programme must be Slovenian music or music production of Slovenian creators. In the opinion of the applicants, this percentage should be that much more than once, and they also propose to increase the shares currently in force when it comes to each individual programme of Radio Television of Slovenia and the share of all broadcasts of radio and television programmes of particular importance. The proposed changes are intended to contribute to the protection of Slovenian language and Slovenian music audiovisual creativity and to the strengthening of national identity. In the Desus Group, we agree with the applicants that the media are important for the development of society and that they also ensure the dissemination of the Slovenian word and help to cultivate patriotism and a positive attitude towards their own country and language. We also attach to the proponents that Slovenian music creativity should be promoted. We therefore agree that a redesign of the area of ensuring the prescribed shares of Slovenian music in radio and television programmes should also be considered. However, we point out that the media area is very complex and that is why it is necessary to take a comprehensive approach to making changes in this area. That is why the Resolution on the National Programme for Culture, which was adopted in November 2013 and is a strategic document of the development planning of cultural policy, provides for a comprehensive revision of media legislation based on a pre-prepared media strategy, to which we have committed ourselves in the coalition treaty. The proposed amendment addresses this area in a non-systematic manner and raises a number of issues, such as the issue of the regulation of effective control and the decision of appropriate sanctions for offenders. Exceptions to the achievement of the quotas should also be provided for. In the Desus Group, we are committed to systematically amending the Media Act, on the basis of preliminary analysis of the situation, and to examining in advance the effects of the introduction of higher shares. Since we believe that the proposed law does not meet these criteria, we will vote against the conclusion that the law is suitable for further consideration. At the same time, we expect the Government to prepare, as soon as possible, a comprehensive amendment to media legislation that will be prepared on the basis of analyses of the state of Slovenian music and audiovisual production. Thank you.
