ParlaMint-SI_2015-01-27-SDZ7-Redna-04.ana.u160	Thank you very much. Have a nice hello again! The proposal for a bill proposed by the SDS is not based on any analysis of the state of Slovenian music and audiovisual production. I think that this would be necessary in particular in the field of Slovenian music production, as the bill provides for twice the current regulatory share of Slovenian music on commercial programmes, half of which is supposed to be Slovenian music, under 5 years. It is not clear from the novel whether the quantity of quality Slovenian music that could possibly have fulfilled such a high prescribed proportion has been analysed. The proposal also does not contain an analysis of the effects of increasing the prescribed proportion of Slovenian music. The reasons for the amendment of the SDS proposal are largely not given, but it states that more precise definitions in the field of the definition of audiovisual works make it possible to monitor and consequently respect the prescribed shares more effectively, while the provisions on control, namely Articles 108 and 109 of the current Media Law, do not change at all. With regard to audiovisual works, the applicant therefore refers to the misuse of the requirement to record and verify compliance with the quotas laid down by the Communications Network and Services Agency of the Republic of Slovenia and notes that the lack of accurate data makes effective control impossible. This reason, of course, is not sufficient for a logical assessment, since only by raising the mandatory quota and the system of control do we change, we do not ensure any qualitative leap in ensuring compliance with the quotas. If the applicant concludes that the reporting on compliance with the statutory regulation on the shares of Slovenian independent and European audiovisual works in the present is misleading, it cannot guarantee that the requirement to raise the share will not continue to be misleading. In comparatively legal work, the applicant cites cases of countries with a much larger music market, which have a significant share of national music in the legislation. In doing so, it is important for the proposer to complement that the larger countries also have a much larger creative pool from which they can draw on quality national music, and that the increase in the minimum share of national music may pose a significant risk to the decline in the quality of music programmes and to the poverty of cultural diversity and, in the long term, even cultural isolationism. Quality creativity can only be born in a culturally diverse environment where there is a rich exchange of different types of music, music from different backgrounds and types. However, if you have already mentioned to compare arrangements in France, let me say that radio stations in France, where the quota is 40%, are complaining that they are difficult to meet, despite having nearly 50 million listeners. The only radio in Slovenia, Radio S, which plays exclusively Slovenian music, does business with loss. The proposal further wrongly states that the amendment has no financial implications for the state budget. The proposal, however, introduces a register of independent producers, which means additional burdens both for existing work obligations, i.e. keeping a register, monitoring and budget, the need for additional employment, as well as for producers themselves, with the obligation to register, payment of a possible fee and so on. It also introduces an additional administrative obstacle, i.e. a new procedure or register. The proposal is also flawed in the case of a provision on the management of the register of independent producers, since it does not specify the procedure or manner in which such register is maintained, the registration and deletion of the register, the communication of amendments and so on. It does not specify the exact conditions for entry into the register. The determination that the register is refreshed by means of associations representing independent producers is not appropriate in terms of management and decision-making in administrative proceedings. We can identify some other shortcomings, such as the lack of criteria for assessing the eligibility of the entry and so on. If I now make some progress in the text of the articles that are being proposed, I will find that in Article 1, the proposed definition, which would define the concept of audiovisual works more precisely, is not clear enough, since the interpretation of the practice so far is not clear enough. A more precise definition of what is included in the required share of Slovenian audiovisual works in the annual broadcast time of the individual issuer is proposed. The aim is to prevent the current situation when issuers include in the quota each audiovisual performance irrespective of the number of repetitions. I do not think that the amendment of the article is appropriate, as it defines audiovisual work as works that are in any way expressed as individual creations in the field of literature, science and art and have been written a synopsis and/or scenario for them. Which means that, elsewhere, audiovisual creatures other than literature, science and art are not audiovisual works. What, for example, is a documentary on a specific social issue? What are video clips? Talk shows? Television shows and stuff? An animated film is also an example, which is not sufficiently covered by this definition, but there is no scenario for it but a storyboard, which is to be taken into account when defining the concept of audiovisual work. Specifying the areas of individual creation is completely unnecessary, these works in this definition are not covered, despite the fact that they correspond to all the professional and other legal definitions of audiovisual work. Indeed, the definition of these parts also contains other existing legislation and that definition would result in conflicting provisions. For example, the Act on the Slovenian Film Centre, the Public Agency of the Republic of Slovenia, which defines audiovisual activity in Article 4, as the activity of all production, postproduction, distribution and display of audiovisual works intended for public performance in recognised technical standards, irrespective of the bearer of image and sound and the extended audiovisual culture. Then there is the Copyright and Related Rights Act, which defines audiovisual works in Article 103, and I will not read this definition now, but it is not the definition that the proposer wants. And I could count it. However, let me say that the proposer in Article 2 of the proposal proposes one thing which I would be happy to support, and I also think is SMC, i.e. the introduction of two time intervals at which these quotas are to be met. This, I think, is a very welcome proposal, and we could, as a matter of fact, support it. In Article 3, where the criteria for the number of shares are added, I believe that this will be very difficult to monitor and that abuses in this way will be even more frequent as the criteria are tightened. In Article 4, however, I consider this to be the most questionable article of all, since the implementation of the legal requirement at the lower threshold of 600 hours of prime Slovenian audiovisual works on RTV Slovenia and at least 150 hours of prime Slovenian audiovisual works by independent producers is in complete disharmony with the budgetary capabilities and some realistic financial frameworks in which Slovenian film or audiovisual production takes place. In fact, the Ministry of Culture has been providing around €3 million per year for the realisation of film and audiovisual works for several years, which is enough to produce a maximum of 5 films, mostly full-time films, and not more than 15 audiovisual works, mostly short films. If we expect that the average minute of Slovenian feature film is about 100 minutes and that audiovisual works are mostly between 30 and 50 minutes long, we can quickly calculate that in Slovenia public money is enough for a few more than 18 hours program of independent producers. If we add up to 1.7 million euros that RTV Slovenia has to devote to cinematographic films after the obligation of the Slovenian Film Centre Act, we can add a maximum of 2 feature films and a few short documentaries each year, i.e. about 5 hours of the programme. We come together for a maximum of 23 hours. The proposal to amend the Media Act is contrary to the Resolution on the National Programme for Culture 2014-2017, adopted by the National Assembly on 27 November 2013 and foresees a comprehensive revision of media legislation on the basis of a pre-prepared media strategy. The proposal only carries some partial solutions, and for some problems it does not offer solutions at all. In particular, solutions are lacking to allow faster and more effective control by the Communications Networks and Services Agency of the Republic of Slovenia and the Cultural and Media Inspectorate and to establish appropriate sanctions for offenders. The law in force does not contain them. The current law was adopted by the National Assembly in 2006. At that time, the government of Janez Janša rejected the proposal of the Slovenian Music Union and ignored their proposal. The union then suggested that at least 30% of all daily music of each radio programme should be Slovenian music or music production of Slovenian creators and creators. For RTV Slovenia and other radio programmes of particular importance, at least 40% were proposed in the band of musicians. And this was not accepted by the then government of John Janša. It therefore follows from the proposal that it was prepared for rapid, superficial, without prior analysis of the situation and effects, it is partial, incomplete and incomplete. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2015-01-27-SDZ7-Redna-04.ana.u190	Thank you very much. Your Honor! First of all, I wanted to save time for all of us, on the other hand, because I'm actually embarrassed to talk about it. [[…]] So, we didn't know about these proposals that came later from RTV Slovenia, and the rest, we prepared these things ourselves and somehow found that we also match what the coalition said today. That is why I thought at first that I would save time for everyone, including the viewers in front of the TV screen, because I think we should start, at least as far as politics is concerned, doing a little differently; but not for a political party to make a flawed law just because it is put into the procedure and then fights between each and the other. I think that each party, regardless of what it is that is actually proposing, should be different from those proposed in this law. However, I would like to say what are the other reasons for rejecting this law, which, of course, cannot be supported because of the many shortcomings that I have already pointed out in my introductory part. Thus, provisions are lacking to allow faster and more effective control, both by the Agency for Communications Networks and Services of the Republic of Slovenia and also by the Slovenian Cultural and Media Inspectorate and to establish appropriate sanctions for offenders. The current media law does not provide for prescribed fines for the failure to achieve the prescribed share of Slovenian audiovisual works, and this law has not been drafted by the current coalition. Greater quotas would significantly increase inflows to organisations such as SAZAS. We understand this interest, but we are working first at the Ministry for the transparent operation of such organisations, which fall within the framework of the Ministry of Economy, Development and Technology. This ministry is therefore actively involved in strengthening the control of SAZAS and other such organisations, where it is only in this way that we can ensure that quotas are, at least, respected or otherwise respected. The applicant submits that with more precise definitions in the field of the definition of audiovisual works it is possible to more effectively monitor and consequently respect the prescribed shares, while the provisions on control, namely Articles 108 and 109 of the Media Act, are not being amended at all. The area of the regulated share of Slovenian music in radio and television programmes remains incomplete, as the proposal for the amendment does not abolish the provisions of the current Media Act, which considers the performance of Slovenian music to be part of its own production. Nor does it solve the entirely clear dilemma which music is considered to be Slovenian music at all: music in Slovenian language or music produced by Slovenian citizens. In Article 1, the applicant amends the second paragraph of Article 68, thereby narrowing the concept of Slovenian audiovisual works to those expressed in any way as individual intellectual services in the field of literature, science and art and has written a synopsis and or a script for them. With this change, a number of audiovisual works, such as video clips, talk shows, television shows and others could no longer be considered as audiovisual works, since they are not synopses and/or scenarios, even though such works correspond to all professional concepts of audiovisual activity or audiovisual work. Instead of unifying the various definitions of audiovisual works, this definition, which is in this amendment of the law, would further confuse existing definitions, which are in the Law on the Slovenian Film Centre, the Law on Copyright and Related Rights and elsewhere. The proposed definitions unduly change and enhance the concept of audiovisual work compared to other regulations in the Republic of Slovenia. Moreover, the concept is not clearly defined. The bill's proposal, as has been said, is not based on any analysis of the state of Slovenian music and audiovisual production. This would be necessary in particular in the field of Slovenian music production, as the proposal for a new law in Article 2 provides for a double increase in the current regulatory share of Slovenian music on commercial programmes, half of which must be Slovenian music, less than 5 years old. The national interest followed by the Ministry is certainly in the performance of Slovenian music; however, it is not said that the Slovenian music market is large enough to provide enough quality music. It should not happen that, in order to comply with the increased prescribed shares, less quality Slovenian music, which is not entirely suitable for the national programme, would be contrary, of course, to the requirements and expectations we have for example, for Radiotelevision Slovenia. This is particularly the case because, according to the proposal of the amendment, at least half the share of Slovenian music should be the music production of Slovenian creators and creators, who are under 5 years of age. And this market is so much narrower. The opening part of the amendment does not contain an analysis of the potential quantity, the quantity of quality Slovenian music that could fill so high a prescribed proportion. In view of the above, the preliminary examination of the proposal for a new law would be even more important and the analysis of the consequences would be even more important. The proposed amendment does not take into account the development of the thematic programmes of Slovenian publishers, where it is particularly necessary to examine whether the prescribed share of domestic production can be achieved, for example in film and documentary thematic programmes. According to some data, this could not be possible, but the applicant does not have any analysis or unsatisfactory answers in order to be accepted or rejected by the contrary. Thus, the purpose or reason for detailed and stricter regulation of the area of regulated shares of Slovenian audiovisual works in RTV Slovenia TV programmes remains only at the level of desire for a greater share of Slovenian works, which by the way is also the aim of the Ministry of Culture. However, the Ministry is aware of its responsibilities for the seriousness of the proposals made, which is why we are convinced that the consequences of the possible introduction of such large shares must be examined first. The fact that the proposals are premature also proves that the applicant does not explain convincingly why, according to the new Slovenian audiovisual works, only premieres and first repetitions are considered, which is, by the way, also a departure from the regulation applicable to European audiovisual works. Such a provision may have financial implications, for example for the regional centres of Television Koper and Television Maribor, which would require a higher contribution to Slovenian audiovisual works and independent production. The proposal for a new law further wrongly states that the bill has no financial implications for the state budget. The proposal introduces a register of independent producers, which entails additional burdens both for existing work obligations, for keeping a register, for monitoring and budgeting, any additional employment needs and for producers themselves the obligation to register – payment of the fee. It also introduces an additional administrative obstacle, introduces a new procedure or register. The proposal for a new law refers in particular to the French and Croatian arrangements from the point of view of comparative arrangements, where the Croatian regime is expected to be identical to the current Slovenian regime and therefore cannot serve as a justification for the proposed amendments. Thus, the applicant remains subject to a French regime which, within the framework of the European Union, is to be regarded as an exception in the field of regulated parts of production in the French language. Therefore, we are missing in the comparative review in addition to the analysis of the situation and effects of at least some other country's case, which could serve as an example of good practice due to comparability with Slovenia. Article 4 of the amendment amends the second paragraph of Article 92 of the Media Act and adds the new sixth paragraph of that Article. Slovenian audiovisual works on the first and second TV programme of Radiotelevision of Slovenia must total at least 25% of the annual broadcasting time, with one quarter or 6.25% of this share – I would remind you here that one quarter of this share is not 6,25% – should be created by independent producers or at least 600 hours of prime Slovenian audiovisual works and at least 150 hours of prime Slovenian audiovisual works by independent producers. It should be examined whether the proposed amendment to the law is feasible, for example, for Radiotelevision of Slovenia, as it appears that it does not annually obtain as many programme audiovisual content as would achieve the proposed mandatory time for premiere broadcasts. In addition, the financial sustainability of the proposed amendment should be examined, as according to some data it would mean that Radiotelevision Slovenia should significantly increase the current means of production if it wants to create, acquire or buy at least 600 hours of prime Slovenian audiovisual works and at least 150 hours of prime Slovenian audiovisual works of independent producers, if we are looking at the narrow definition of Slovenian audiovisual works, as stated in the proposal for the amendment. The quantitative increase in the mandatory share of appropriate or new audiovisual works does not at the same time guarantee an increase in quality, but can, as we have already stressed, result in a reduction in the quality of the programme. This is contrary to the public interest, which is why Radiotelevision Slovenia is established by law at all. Once again, I would like to draw attention to the error in the proposal for a second paragraph, since 6,25 % is not one quarter of the share. Paragraph 6 provides that the Ministry of Culture shall maintain a register of independent producers, which shall be updated twice a year by means of associations representing independent producers. The proposal for a law is also nominally flawed, in particular in the case of a provision for the maintenance of a register of independent producers. The draft law does not specify the procedure or manner in which such register is maintained, the registration and deletion of the register, the communication of amendments and so on. It does not specify the conditions for registration in the register, nor does it provide that the register is updated by means of associations representing independent producers, and in terms of management and decision-making in administrative procedures is entirely inappropriate. However, there are other shortcomings in the proposal for a new law, such as the lack of criteria for assessing the eligibility of entry into the register. Thus, there are no provisions on the criteria or the possibility of assessing the country's eligibility of the producer to independent producer status. A transitional provision is also not technically appropriate for the application of the amended third paragraph of Article 86 at the latest two years after the entry into force of this Act, as this would mean that it could be applied at any time within two years. This is unacceptable from the point of view of legal certainty, since the time of application of the provisions or regulations must be fully established. It follows from the proposal for a new law that the proposed solutions have been prepared quickly, without a preliminary analysis of the situation, incomplete and incomplete, as they address only specific areas of media regulation and also do not address all relevant issues within specific areas and their justification is very short and does not provide a comprehensive basis for an effective impact assessment. Let me get on with it. The proposal for a new law is not in line, as we said, with the National Programme for Culture 2014-2017, which envisages a comprehensive revision of media legislation rather than partial solutions. From SDS Members you also voted in favour of the first signed Jože Tanko, Andrej Chuš, mag. Branko Grimes, Tink Lah, mag. Marko Pogačnik and Ljubo Žnidar. The proposal for a new law is also not in line with the decision of the National Assembly of the Republic of Slovenia, the Committee on Culture, Education, Sport and Youth, adopted on 6 October 2009, which states: "The strategy for the development of radio and television programmes in the Republic of Slovenia should be created between the Broadcasting Council, the APEC, publishers and the Ministry of Culture. The strategy should be the basis for preparing changes and supplementing the media law." The proposals for this Decision were: Franc Pukšič, SDS or SDS. Finally, it should be mentioned that the proposal for a law was tabled on 16 January this year and is today, 9 days later, at first reading. It is problematic that the political party that has tabled the proposal for a new law has not invited the professional and other public to make their views and comments, not from the point of view of the procedure, but from the point of view of what each law is supposed to contain, that is to say, the treatment also, as regards the professional and lay public, of course interested, is a major flaw. I think that's a problem, too. It also follows from my comments on the proposal for a new law, which I have seen in the meantime, that there was not enough time to involve the professional and other public in the debate on the proposal for a new law. The fact that the expert and other public do not have the opportunity to comment on the proposal for a new law already falls within the field of transparency or that the applicant does not wish or does not consider it important for the public to define themselves in general as to the solutions it proposes. Regardless of everything that has already been said, I would like to thank the proposed amendment of the law for its good intentions and also for the constructive proposals in terms of direction towards increasing the regulatory shares of Slovenian production, which will certainly be examined in the preparation of comprehensive amendments to media legislation.
